 
 
 
 [math/0109173] Rough solutions of the Einstein-Vacuum equations 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

 
 


 
 
 
 
 
 
 Rough solutions of the Einstein-Vacuum equations 
 
 
 Sergiu Klainerman
 
 Address:  Department of Mathematics, Princeton University,
Princeton NJ 08544
 
 Email address: seri@@math.princeton.edu 
 
 and 
 Igor Rodnianski
 
 Address:  Department of Mathematics, Princeton University,
Princeton NJ 08544
 
 Email address: irod@@math.princeton.edu 
 

 Abstract. 
 
 This is the first in a series of papers in which
we initiate the study of very rough solutions to the initial value problem
for the Einstein vacuum equations expressed relative
to wave coordinates. By very rough we mean solutions
which cannot be constructed by the classical techniques of energy estimates
and Sobolev inequalities. Following [ Kl-Ro ] we develop new
analytic methods based on Strichartz type inequalities which results in a gain of
half a derivative relative to the classical result. Our methods blend paradifferential
techniques with a geometric approach to the derivation of decay estimates. The
latter allows
us to take full advantage of the specific structure of the Einstein equations. 
 
 
 1991 Mathematics Subject Classification 35J10

 We consider the Einstein Vacuum equations,

 Remark 1.2 . 
 
 Theorem 1.1 implies the classical local existence result of
 [ H-K-M ] for asymptotically flat initial data sets Σ , g , k \Sigma,g,k with
 ∇ g , k ∈ H s − 1 ​ ( Σ ) \nabla g,k\in H^{s-1}(\Sigma) and s > 5 2 s>\frac{5}{2} , relative to a fixed
system of coordinates. Uniqueness can be
proved for additional regularity s > 1 + 5 2 s>1+\frac{5}{2} .
We recall that an initial data set ( Σ , g , k ) (\Sigma,g,k) 
consists of
a three dimensional complete Riemannian manifold

the scalar curvature of ( Σ , g ) (\Sigma,g) .

is said to be asymptotically flat (AF) if there exists a system of coordinates

defined in a neighborhood of infinity 3 3

relative to which the metric

mass theorem M ≥ 0 M\geq 0 and M = 0 M=0 implies that the initial data set is flat. Because
of the mass term we cannot assume that g − e ∈ L 2 ​ ( Σ ) g-e\in L^{2}(\Sigma) , with e e the 3 3 D Euclidean

 Remark 1.3 . 
 
 The Main Theorem ought to imply existence and uniqueness 5 5 
 5 
 
 
 Properly speaking uniqueness holds, with s > 2 s>2 , only for the reduced equations.
Uniqueness for the actual Einstein equations requires one more derivative,
see [ H-K-M ] . for initial conditions with H s H^{s} , s > 2 s>2 , regularity. To
achieve this we only need to approximate a given H s H^{s} initial data set( i.e.
 ∇ g ∈ H s − 1 ​ ( Σ ) , k ∈ H s − 1 ​ ( Σ ) \nabla g\in H^{s-1}(\Sigma),k\in H^{s-1}(\Sigma) , s > 2 s>2 ) for
the Einstein vacuum equations by classical initial data sets, i.e. H s ′ H^{s^{\prime}} 
data sets with
 s ′ > 5 2 s^{\prime}>\frac{5}{2} , for which theorem 1.1 holds. The Main Theorem allows
us to pass to the limit and derive existence of solutions for the given, rough,
initial data set. We don’t know however if such an approximation result
for the constraint equations exists in the literature.

 for some c > 0 c>0 . 
 
 
 The classical local existence result for systems of wave equations
of type ( 6 ) is based on energy estimates and the standard
 H s ⊂ L ∞ H^{s}\subset L^{\infty} Sobolev inequality. Indeed using energy estimates and simple
commutation inequalities one can show that, 
 

 
 (9) 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( t ) ‖ H s − 1 ≤ E ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ H s − 1 \|\partial\phi(t)\|_{H^{s-1}}\leq E\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{H^{s-1}} 
 
 
 with a constant E E , 
 

 
 (10) 
 
 E = exp ⁡ ( C ​ ∫ 0 t ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( τ ) ‖ L x ∞ ​ 𝑑 τ ) E=\exp\bigg(C\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial\phi(\tau)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}d\tau\bigg) 
 
 
 By the classical Sobolev inequality, 
 

 
 
 E ≤ exp ⁡ ( C ​ t ​ sup 0 ≤ τ ≤ t ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( τ ) ‖ H s − 1 ​ d ​ τ ) E\leq\exp\bigg(Ct\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq t}\|\partial\phi(\tau)\|_{H^{s-1}}d\tau\bigg) 
 
 
 provided that s > 5 2 s>\frac{5}{2} . The classical local existence result follows
by combining this last estimate, for a small time
interval, with the energy estimates
( 9 ). 
 
 
 This scheme is very wasteful. To do better one would like
to take advantage of the mixed L t 1 ​ L x ∞ L_{t}^{1}L_{x}^{\infty} norm appearing on the right hand
side of ( 10 ). Unfortunately there are no good estimates for such norms
even when ϕ \phi is simply a solution of the standard wave equation 
 

 
 (11) 
 
 □ ​ ϕ = 0 \square\phi=0 
 
 
 in Minkowski space.
There exist however improved regularity estimates
for solutions of ( 11 ) in the mixed L t 2 ​ L x ∞ L_{t}^{2}L_{x}^{\infty} norm .
More precisely, if ϕ \phi is a solution of ( 11 ) and ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 
arbitrarily small, 
 

 
 (12) 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L t 2 ​ L x ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , T ] × ℝ 3 ) ≤ C ​ T ϵ ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ H 1 + ϵ . \|\partial\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}L_{x}^{\infty}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3})}\leq CT^{\epsilon}\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{H^{1+\epsilon}}. 
 
 
 Based on this fact it was reasonable to hope that
one can improve the Sobolev exponent in the classical local existence theorem from
 s > 5 2 s>\frac{5}{2} to s > 2 s>2 . This can be easily done for solutions of
semilinear equations, see [ Po-Si ] . In the quasilinear case, however, the situation
is far more difficult. One can no longer rely on the Strichartz
inequality ( 12 ) for the flat D’Alembertian in
( 11 ); we need instead its extension to the operator
 𝐠 α ​ β ∂ α ∂ β {\bf g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta} appearing in ( 6 ). Moreover, since
the metric 𝐠 α ​ β {\bf g}^{\alpha\beta} depends on the solution ϕ \phi , it can have
only as much regularity as ϕ \phi itself. This means that we have
to confront the issue of proving Strichartz estimates for wave
operators g α ​ β ∂ α ∂ β g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta} with very rough coefficients 𝐠 α ​ β {\bf g}^{\alpha\beta} .
This issue was recently addressed in the pioneering works of Smith [ Sm ] ,
Bahouri-Chemin [ Ba-Ch1 ] , [ Ba-Ch2 ] and Tataru [ Ta1 ] , [ Ta2 ] , we refer to
the introduction in
 [ Kl1 ] and
 [ Kl-Ro ] for a more thorough discussion of their important contributions. 
 
 
 The results of Bahouri-Chemin and Tataru are based on establishing
a Strichartz type inequality, with a loss , for wave operators
with very rough
coefficients 6 6 
 6 
 
 
 The derivatives of the coefficients
 𝐠 {\bf g} are required to be bounded in L t ∞ ​ H x s − 1 L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{s-1} 
and L t 2 ​ L x ∞ L_{t}^{2}L_{x}^{\infty} norms, with s s compatible with the regularity
required on the
right hand side of the Strichartz inequality one wants to prove. .
The optimal result 7 7 
 7 
 
 
 Recently Smith-Tataru [ Sm-Ta ] 
have shown that the result of Tataru is indeed sharp. in this regard,
due to Tataru, see [ Ta2 ] , requires a loss of σ = 1 6 \sigma=\frac{1}{6} .
This leads to a proof of local well posedness for systems of type
( 6 ) with s > 2 + 1 6 s>2+\frac{1}{6} . 
 
 
 To do better than that one needs
to take into account the nonlinear structure of the equations. In
 [ Kl-Ro ] we were able to improve the result of Tataru by
taking into account not only the expected regularity properties of
the coefficients g α ​ β g^{\alpha\beta} in ( 6 ) but also the fact that they
are themselves solutions to a similar system of equations. This
allowed us to improve the exponent s s ,
needed in the proof of well posedness of equations of type 8 8 
 8 
 
 
 The result in
 [ Kl-Ro ] applies to general equations of type ( 6 ) not necessarily tied
to ( 1 ). In [ Kl-Ro ] we have also made the simplifying assumptions 𝐧 = 1 {\bf n}=1 
and 𝐯 = 0 {\bf v}=0 . 
( 6 ), to
 s > 2 + 2 − 3 2 s>2+\frac{2-\sqrt{3}}{2} . Our approach was based on a combination of the paradifferential
calculus ideas, initiated in [ Ba-Ch1 ] and [ Ta2 ] , with a geometric treatment of
the actual equations introduced in [ Kl1 ] . The main improvement was due to a gain of
conormal differentiability for solutions to the Eikonal equations 
 

 
 (13) 
 
 H α ​ β ​ ∂ α u ​ ∂ β u = 0 H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}u=0 
 
 
 where the background metric H H 
is a properly microlocalized and rescaled version
of the metric 𝐠 α ​ β {\bf g}^{\alpha\beta} in ( 6 ).
That gain could be traced down to the fact
that a certain component of the Ricci curvature of H H 
has a special form. More precisely denoting by L ′ L^{\prime} the null
geodesic vectorfield associated to u u , L ′ = − H α ​ β ∂ β u ∂ α L^{\prime}=-H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}u\partial_{\alpha} , and
rescaling it
in an appropriate fashion 9 9 
 9 
 
 
 such < L , T > H = 1 <L,T>_{H}=1 with T T is the unit normal
to the level hypersurfaces Σ t \Sigma_{t} associated to the time function t t , , L = b ​ L ′ L=bL^{\prime} ,
we found that the 𝐑 L ​ L = {\bf R}_{LL}= Ric ( H ) ​ ( L , L ) (H)(L,L) , verifies the remarkable
identity: 
 

 
 (14) 
 
 𝐑 L ​ L = L ⁡ ( z ) − 1 2 ​ L μ ​ L ν ​ ( H α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β H μ ​ ν ) + e {\bf R}_{LL}=L(z)-\frac{1}{2}L^{\mu}L^{\nu}(H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}H_{\mu\nu})+{\mbox{e}} 
 
 
 where z ≤ O ⁡ ( | ∂ H | ) z\leq O(|\partial H|) and e ≤ O ⁡ ( | ∂ H | 2 ) e\leq O(|\partial H|^{2}) . Thus, apart from L ⁡ ( z ) L(z) which
is to be integrated along the null geodesic flow generated by L L , the only
terms which depend of the second derivatives of H H appear in H α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β H H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}H and can
therefore be eliminated with the help of the equations ( 6 ). 
 
 
 In this paper we develop the ideas of [ Kl-Ro ] further by taking full advantage of the
Einstein equations ( 1 )
in wave coordinates ( 6 ). An important aspect of our analysis here
is that the term L ⁡ ( z ) L(z) appearing on the right hand side of ( 14 )
vanishes identically. We make use of both the vanishing of the Ricci curvature
of 𝐠 {\bf g} and the wave coordinate condition ( 2 ). The other important
new features are the use of energy estimates along the null hypersurfaces
generated by the optical function u u and a more efficient use of the conormal
properties of the null structure equations. 
 
 
 Our work is divided in three parts. In this paper we give all the details
in the proof of the Main Theorem with the exception of those results which
concern the asymptotic properties of the Ricci coefficients( the Asymptotics Theorem),
the isoperimetric and trace inequalities on 2-surfaces.
We give precise statements of these results in section 4.
Our second paper [ Kl-Ro2 ] is dedicated to the proof of the
Asymptotics Theorem. The isoperimetric and trace inequalities
together with some other results needed in [ Kl-Ro2 ] are
proved in our third paper [ Kl-Ro3 ] . 
 
 
 We strongly believe that the result of our main theorem is not sharp.
The critical Sobolev exponent for the Einstein equations is s c = 3 2 s_{c}=\frac{3}{2} .
A proof of well posedness for s = s c s=s_{c} will provide a much stronger version
of the global stability of Minkowski space than that of [ Ch-Kl ] .
This is completely out of reach at the present time. A more reasonable goal,
at the present time, is to prove the L 2 L^{2} - curvature conjecture , see
 [ Kl2 ] , corresponding to the exponent s = 2 s=2 . 
 
 
 
 2. reduction to decay estimates 

 
 The proof of the main theorem can be reduced to a microlocal
decay estimate. The reduction is standard 10 10 
 10 
 
 
 see
 [ Kl-Ro ] and the references therein ; we quickly review
here the main steps. The precise statements and their proofs are given
in section 8. 
 
 
 
 • 
 
 Energy estimates 
 
 
 Assuming that ϕ \phi 
is a solution 11 11 
 11 
 
 
 i.e. a classical
solution according to theorem 1.1 . of ( 6 ) on [ 0 , T ] × ℝ 3 [0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3} 
we have the
apriori energy estimate: 
 
 
 

 
 (15) 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] ∞ ​ H ˙ s − 1 ≤ C ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ H ˙ s − 1 \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}\dot{H}^{s-1}}\leq C\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{\dot{H}^{s-1}} 
 
 
 with a constant C C depending only on
 ‖ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] ∞ ​ L x ∞ \|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}L_{x}^{\infty}} 
and ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 1 ​ L x ∞ \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{1}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}} . 
 
 • 
 
 Strichartz estimate 
To prove our Main Theorem we need, in addition to ( 15 )
an estimate of the form: 
 

 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 1 ​ L x ∞ ≤ C ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ H s − 1 \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{1}_{[0,T]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\leq C\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{H^{s-1}} 
 
 
 for any s > 2 s>2 . We accomplish it by establishing a Strichartz type
inequality of the form, 
 

 
 (16) 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 2 ​ L x ∞ ≤ C ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ H 1 + γ \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\leq C\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{H^{1+\gamma}} 
 
 
 with any fixed γ > 0 \gamma>0 . We achieve this with the help of a bootstrap
argument. More precisely we make the assumption 
 
 
 

 
 (17) 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] ∞ ​ H 1 + γ + ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 2 ​ L x ∞ ≤ B 0 , \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}H^{1+\gamma}}+\|\partial\phi\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq B_{0}, 
 
 
 and use it to prove the
better estimate; 
 
 
 

 
 (18) 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 2 ​ L x ∞ ≤ C ⁡ ( B 0 ) ​ T δ . \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C(B_{0})\,T^{\delta}. 
 
 
 for some δ > 0 \delta>0 . Thus, for sufficiently small T > 0 T>0 , we find that
( 16 ) holds true. 
 
 • 
 
 Proof of the Main Theorem 
 
 
 This can be done easily by combining the energy estimates with
the Strichartz estimate stated above.

 with ∑ λ c λ ≤ 1 \sum_{\lambda}c_{\lambda}\leq 1 .

 Reduction to an L 1 − L ∞ L^{1}-L^{\infty} decay estimate 
 
 
 The standard way to prove a Strichartz inequality of the type
discussed above is to reduce it, by a T ​ T ∗ TT^{*} type argument,
to an L 1 − L ∞ L^{1}-L^{\infty} dispersive type inequality. The inequality we need,
concerning the initial value problem 
 

 
 
 □ H ( λ ) ​ ψ = 1 | H ( λ ) | ​ ∂ α ( H ( λ ) α ​ β ​ | H ( λ ) | ​ ∂ β ψ ) = 0 , \square_{H_{(\lambda)}}\psi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|H_{(\lambda)}|}}\partial_{\alpha}\big(H^{\alpha\beta}_{(\lambda)}\sqrt{|H_{(\lambda)}|}\,\partial_{\beta}\psi\big)=0, 
 
 
 with data at t = t 0 t=t_{0} 
has the form, 
 

 
 
 ∥ P ∂ ψ ( t ) ∥ L x ∞ ≤ C ( B 0 ) ( 1 ( 1 + | t − t 0 | ) 1 − δ + d ( t ) ) ∑ k = 0 m ∥ ∇ k ∂ ψ ( t 0 ) ∥ L x 1 \|P\,\partial\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\,C(B_{0})\bigg(\frac{1}{(1+|t-t_{0}|)^{1-\delta}}+d(t)\bigg)\sum_{k=0}^{m}\|\nabla^{k}\partial\psi(t_{0})\|_{L^{1}_{x}} 
 
 
 for some integer m ≥ 0 m\geq 0 . 
 
 • 
 
 Final reduction to a localized L 2 − L ∞ L^{2}-L^{\infty} decay estimate

Assume that the initial data is given at t = t 0 ∈ [ 0 , t ∗ ] t=t_{0}\in[0,t_{*}] ,

 Remark 2.2 . 
 
 In view of the proof of the Main Theorem
presented above, which relies on the
final estimate ( 18 ), we can in
what follows treat the bootstrap constant B 0 B_{0} as a universal constant
and bury
the dependence on it
in the notation ≲ \lesssim we introduce below. 
 
 
 
 Definition 2.3 . 
 
 We use the notation A ≲ B A\lesssim B to express
the inequality A ≤ C ​ B A\leq CB with a universal constant, which
may depend on B 0 B_{0} and various other parameters
depending only on B 0 B_{0} introduced
in the proof. 
 
 
 
 The proof of theorem 21 relies on a
generalized Morawetz type energy estimate which will be presented in the next section.
We shall in fact construct a vectorfield, analogous to the Morawetz vectorfield
in the Minkowski space, which depends heavily on the “background metric”
 H = H ( λ ) H=H_{(\lambda)} . In the next proposition we display most of the main properties
of the metric

For all t 0 ≤ t ≤ t ∗ \,\,t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{*} , ψ ⁡ ( t ) \psi(t) is supported
in 𝒟 t 0 − 1 ⊂ 𝒟 0 {\mathcal{D}}_{t_{0}-1}\subset{\mathcal{D}}_{0} and 
 

 
 
 𝒬 ⁡ [ ψ ] ​ ( t ) ≲ 𝒬 ⁡ [ ψ ] ​ ( t 0 ) . {\cal Q}[\psi](t)\lesssim{\cal Q}[\psi](t_{0}).

 7. Proof of the L 2 − L ∞ L^{2}-L^{\infty} decay estimate; theorem 21 

 
 In this section we rely on the Boundedness Theorem 3.3 to prove the
crucial theorem 21 . 
 
 
 Recall that ℰ ⁡ [ ψ ] = ℰ i ​ [ ψ ] + ℰ e ​ [ ψ ] {\mathcal{E}}[\psi]={\mathcal{E}}^{i}[\psi]+{\mathcal{E}}^{e}[\psi] , where

 with a cut-off function ζ \zeta equal to 1 1 in the region u ≤ t 2 u\leq\frac{t}{2} . 
 
 
 Estimate for ( 1 − ζ ) ​ P ​ ψ (1-\zeta)P\psi : 
 
 
 Observe that since the projector P P is an averaging operator on the scale
of size 1 and ( 1 − ζ ) (1-\zeta) is a cut-off function with the scale of size t ≥ 1 t\geq 1 ,
we can essentially write that ( 1 − ζ ) ​ P ​ ψ ≈ P ⁡ ( ψ ⁡ ( 1 − ζ ) ) (1-\zeta)P\psi\approx P(\psi(1-\zeta)) .
Thus the Bernstein inequality, followed
by the
fact ∥ ( 1 − ζ ) ∇ ψ ) ∥ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) ≤ t − 1 ℰ 1 2 [ ψ ] ( t ) \|(1-\zeta)\nabla\psi\big)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\leq t^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}[\psi](t) 
and | ∇ ζ | ≲ t − 1 |\nabla\zeta|\lesssim t^{-1} ,
implies that 
 

 
 (96) 
 
 ‖ P ⁡ ( ψ ⁡ ( t ) ) ​ ( 1 − ζ ) ‖ L x ∞ ≲ ‖ ∇ ( ψ ⁡ ( 1 − ζ ) ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) ≤ t − 1 ​ ℰ 1 2 ​ [ ψ ] ​ ( t ) \|P(\psi(t))(1-\zeta)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|\nabla\big(\psi(1-\zeta)\big)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\leq t^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}[\psi](t) 
 
 
 as desired. 
 
 
 Estimate for ζ ​ P ​ ψ \zeta P\psi\, :  It clearly suffices to establish the estimate for
 P ​ ψ ​ ( t , x ) P\psi(t,x) at any point ( t , x ) (t,x) with
 0 ≤ u ≤ t 4 0\leq u\leq\frac{t}{4} .
According to the Sobolev inequality ( 64 ), with p = 4 p=4 , of proposition 67 
we have
for any positive δ < 1 \delta<1 ,

 8. Proof of the reduction steps 

 
 In this section we give precise statements and proofs for the reduction
steps discussed in section 2. Recall the equation ( 3 ), written
in the form ( 6 ), 
 

 
 (101) 
 
 𝐠 α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ = N ⁡ ( ϕ , ∂ ϕ ) {\bf g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi=N(\phi,\partial\phi)

and 𝐠 α ​ β = 𝐠 α ​ β ​ ( ϕ ) {\bf g}^{\alpha\beta}={\bf g}^{\alpha\beta}(\phi) . In fact ( 𝐠 α ​ β ) = ϕ − 1 ({\bf g}^{\alpha\beta})=\phi^{-1} . We consider solutions
 ϕ \phi of ( 101 ) such that the components of both ϕ \phi and ϕ − 1 \phi^{-1} 
are uniformly bounded. Moreover 𝐠 μ ​ ν {\bf g}_{\mu\nu} approach the Minkowski metric 𝐦 μ ​ ν {\bf m}_{\mu\nu} 
at infinity according to ( 5 ). To avoid repeating this statement in what follows
we introduce the following notation: 
 
 
 Definition 8.1 .

the frequency property. 
 
 
 
 Proof of lemma 8.7 
 
 
 The proof of the lemma is based on the technique of the paradifferential calculus and
is standard 27 27 
 27 
 
 
 The equations discussed in the literature
are somewhat different from the one treated here because of the
non triviality of the components 𝐠 00 {\bf g}^{00} and 𝐠 0 ​ i {\bf g}^{0i} of the metric. This adds only
minor technical complications. . For the sake of completeness
we provide an outline of the arguments. For a more detailed treatment see for example
 [ Ba-Ch1 ] or [ Kl-Ro ] . 
 
 
 Recall that P λ P_{\lambda} denotes the projection on the frequencies of size λ \lambda ,
so that ϕ λ = P λ ​ ϕ \phi^{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}\phi .
We write the equation 𝐠 α ​ β ​ ( ϕ ) ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ = N {\bf g}^{\alpha\beta}(\phi)\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi=N in the form
 − ∂ t 2 ϕ + 𝐧 2 𝐠 0 ​ i ( ϕ ) ∂ t ∂ i ϕ + 𝐧 2 𝐠 i ​ j ( ϕ ) ∂ i ∂ j ϕ = 𝐧 2 N -\partial^{2}_{t}\phi+{\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{0i}(\phi)\partial_{t}\partial_{i}\phi+{\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{ij}(\phi)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\phi={\bf n}^{2}N .
Then 
 

 
 
 − ∂ t 2 ϕ λ + P λ ( 𝐧 2 𝐠 0 ​ i ( ϕ ) ∂ t ∂ i ϕ ) + P λ ( 𝐧 2 𝐠 i ​ j ( ϕ ) ∂ i ∂ j ϕ ) = P λ ( 𝐧 2 N ) . -\partial^{2}_{t}\phi^{\lambda}+P_{\lambda}({\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{0i}(\phi)\partial_{t}\partial_{i}\phi)+P_{\lambda}({\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{ij}(\phi)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\phi)=P_{\lambda}({\bf n}^{2}N). 
 
 
 For convenience we introduce 
 

 
 (106) 
 
 G ⋅ ∂ 2 ϕ = 𝐧 2 ​ 𝐠 0 ​ i ​ ( ϕ ) ​ ∂ t ∂ i ϕ + 𝐧 2 ​ 𝐠 i ​ j ​ ( ϕ ) ​ ∂ i ∂ j ϕ G\cdot\partial^{2}\phi={\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{0i}(\phi)\partial_{t}\partial_{i}\phi+{\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{ij}(\phi)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\phi 
 
 
 and note that at least one of the derivatives on the right hand-side
is a spatial derivative.
Then 
 

 
 
 
 P λ ​ ( G ⋅ ∂ 2 ϕ ) = P λ ​ ∑ μ , ν G μ ⋅ ∂ 2 ϕ ν = P λ ​ ∑ μ < 1 2 ​ ν , ν G μ ⋅ ∂ 2 ϕ ν + \displaystyle P_{\lambda}(G\cdot\partial^{2}\phi)=P_{\lambda}\sum_{\mu,\nu}G^{\mu}\cdot\partial^{2}\phi^{\nu}=P_{\lambda}\sum_{\mu<{\frac{1}{2}}\nu,\nu}G^{\mu}\cdot\partial^{2}\phi^{\nu}+

 It is clear that in the case when of one frequencies μ \mu or ν \nu 
dominate, the projection P λ P_{\lambda} on the frequencies of size λ \lambda 
forces the dominant frequency to be of the same size.
We say that μ ∼ λ \mu\sim\lambda if 1 4 ​ λ ≤ μ ≤ 4 ​ λ \frac{1}{4}\lambda\leq\mu\leq 4\lambda .

 𝐠 < λ α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ < λ = F λ . {\bf g}^{\alpha\beta}_{<\lambda}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi_{<\lambda}=F_{\lambda}.

 The function 𝐠 = ϕ − 1 {\bf g}=\phi^{-1} satisfies similar equations.

 To finish the proof of the proposition 102 we
choose a large parameter Λ \Lambda in such a way that for any

This is always possible
since P < λ P_{<\lambda} is an approximation of the identity and the
original metric ( 𝐧 2 ​ 𝐠 i ​ j ) ({\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{ij}) is uniformly elliptic in [ 0 , T ] [0,T] . 
 
 
 For the values of the dyadic parameter λ ≤ Λ \lambda\leq\Lambda rewrite the
equation for ϕ λ \phi^{\lambda} in the form 
 

 
 
 − ∂ t 2 ϕ λ + ( 𝐧 2 𝐠 0 ​ i ) < λ ∂ t ∂ i ϕ λ + ( 𝐧 2 𝐠 i ​ j ) < λ ∂ i ∂ j ϕ λ = R λ ′ -\partial^{2}_{t}\phi^{\lambda}+({\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{0i})_{<\lambda}\partial_{t}\partial_{i}\phi^{\lambda}+({\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{ij})_{<\lambda}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\phi^{\lambda}=R_{\lambda}^{\prime} 
 
 
 noting that the change of the metric introduces the error term
of the type E 2 E_{2} . 
 
 
 For λ ≥ Λ \lambda\geq\Lambda we keep the form of the equation as in lemma 8.7 
 

 
 
 − ∂ t 2 ϕ λ + ( 𝐧 2 𝐠 0 ​ i ) < λ ∂ t ∂ i ϕ λ + ( 𝐧 2 𝐠 i ​ j ) < λ ∂ i ∂ j ϕ λ = R λ -\partial^{2}_{t}\phi^{\lambda}+({\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{0i})_{<\lambda}\partial_{t}\partial_{i}\phi^{\lambda}+({\bf n}^{2}{\bf g}^{ij})_{<\lambda}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\phi^{\lambda}=R_{\lambda} 
 
 
 In either case, the standard H 1 H^{1} energy estimate for the wave equation
yields 
 

 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ λ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] ∞ ​ L 2 ≤ C ⁡ ( Λ 0 ) ​ ( ‖ ∂ ϕ λ ​ ( 0 ) ‖ L 2 + ‖ R λ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 1 ​ L x 2 ) . \|\partial\phi^{\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}L^{2}}\leq C(\Lambda_{0})(\|\partial\phi^{\lambda}(0)\|_{L^{2}}+\|R_{\lambda}\|_{L^{1}_{[0,T]}L^{2}_{x}}). 
 
 
 
 
 Using lemma 8.7 and the Gronwall inequality we immediately obtain for s > 1 s>1 
 

 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] ∞ ​ H ˙ s − 1 ≲ exp ⁡ ( ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 1 ​ L x ∞ ) ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ H ˙ s − 1 . \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}\dot{H}^{s-1}}\lesssim\exp{(\|\partial\phi\|_{L^{1}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}})}\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{\dot{H}^{s-1}}. 
 
 
 The estimate for s = 1 s=1 follows by standard energy
estimates without the paradifferential
decomposition. 
 
 
 
 8.10. Reduction to the Strichartz type estimates 

 
 As discussed in section 2 we need to prove the Strichartz type inequality
( 16 ). This is achieved by the following 
 
 
 Theorem 8.11 ( A1). 
 
 Let ϕ ∈ C ⁡ ( [ 0 , T ] , 𝐦 + ℍ 1 + γ ) \phi\in C([0,T];{\bf m}+{\mathbb{H}}^{1+\gamma}) be a solution of ( 101 )
on the time interval [ 0 , T ] [0,T] , T ≤ 1 T\leq 1 .
Assume that 
 

 
 (108) 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] ∞ ​ H 1 + γ + ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 2 ​ L x ∞ ≤ B 0 , \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T]}H^{1+\gamma}}+\|\partial\phi\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq B_{0}, 
 
 
 
 
 There exists a small positive exponent δ = δ ⁡ ( B 0 ) \delta=\delta(B_{0}) such that
 ϕ \phi satisfies the following local in time Strichartz type estimate, 
 

 
 (109) 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 2 ​ L x ∞ ≤ C ⁡ ( B 0 ) ​ T δ \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C(B_{0})\,T^{\delta} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 8.12 . 
 
 In view of the remark
 2.2 and definition 2.3 
we shall treat B 0 B_{0} as a universal constant in what follows
and hide the dependence on it in the notation ≲ \lesssim . 
 
 
 
 
 8.13. The dyadic version of
the Strichartz type estimate 

 
 Fix a large frequency parameter Λ \Lambda . It easily follows from the triangle
inequality that
for p ∈ [ 1 , ∞ ] p\in[1,\infty] , 
 

 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L x p ≤ ‖ ∂ ϕ l ​ e ​ Λ ‖ L x p + ∑ λ > Λ ‖ ∂ ϕ λ ‖ L x p . \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\leq\|\partial\phi_{le\Lambda}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}+\sum_{\lambda>\Lambda}\|\partial\phi^{\lambda}\|_{L^{p}_{x}}. 
 
 
 Thus, theorem 109 follows from the following dyadic version of the Strichartz
type estimates for ϕ λ = P λ ​ ϕ \phi^{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}\phi . 
 
 
 Theorem 8.14 ( A2). 
 
 Let ϕ \phi be as in
theorem 109 .
There exists a small positive exponent δ = δ ⁡ ( B 0 ) \delta=\delta(B_{0}) such that
for each λ ≥ Λ \lambda\geq\Lambda , the function
 ϕ λ \phi^{\lambda} satisfies the Strichartz type estimate 
 

 
 (110) 
 
 ‖ ∂ ϕ λ ‖ L [ 0 , T ] 2 ​ L x ∞ ≲ c λ ​ T δ \|\partial\phi^{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\,c_{\lambda}T^{\delta} 
 
 
 with constants c λ c_{\lambda} such that ∑ λ c λ ≤ 1 \sum_{\lambda}c_{\lambda}\leq 1 . 
 
 
 
 Remark 8.15 . 
 
 The corresponding
estimate for small frequencies, i.e. for ϕ < λ \phi_{<\lambda} , follows trivially from the
Sobolev inequality,

 Since Λ \Lambda is a fixed
large parameter, which could depend only upon B 0 B_{0} , we have the desired bound
for the low frequency part of ϕ \phi .

 8.21. Properties of the metric 𝐠 < λ {\bf g}_{<\lambda} 

 
 Recall that 𝐠 < λ μ ​ ν = P ≤ 2 − M 0 ​ λ ​ ( 𝐠 μ ​ ν ) {\bf g}^{\mu\nu}_{<\lambda}=P_{\leq 2^{-M_{0}}\lambda}({\bf g}^{\mu\nu}) where
 𝐠 μ ​ ν {\bf g}^{\mu\nu} is the inverse of the Lorentz metric 𝐠 μ ​ ν = ϕ {\bf g}_{\mu\nu}=\phi .
We shall use the notation 𝐠 < λ {\bf g}_{<\lambda} to denote the inverse
of 𝐠 < λ μ ​ ν {\bf g}^{\mu\nu}_{<\lambda} . Observe that, in view of our assumption λ ≥ Λ \lambda\geq\Lambda ,
 𝐠 < λ {\bf g}_{<\lambda} defines a Lorentz metric in our spacetime region [ 0 , T ] × ℝ 3 [0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3} .
It clearly depends on the solution ϕ \phi of
the quasilinear problem ( 101 ).
In the next proposition we state the properties of
the family 𝐠 < λ {\bf g}_{<\lambda} which follow from the bootstrap condition ( 108 )
on ϕ \phi . We denote by 𝐑 α ​ β ​ ( 𝐠 < λ ) {\bf R}_{\alpha\beta}({\bf g}_{<\lambda}) the components of
Ricci curvature of the metric 𝐠 < λ {\bf g}_{<\lambda} .

 Remark 8.23 . 
 
 It suffices to prove the above estimates
for the inverse metric 𝐠 < λ μ ​ ν = P < λ ​ ( 𝐠 μ ​ ν ) {\bf g}_{<\lambda}^{\mu\nu}=P_{<\lambda}({\bf g}^{\mu\nu}) .
This can be easily seen by Leibnitz rule and the non degeneracy of 𝐠 < λ {\bf g}_{<\lambda} .
On the other hand, due to the explicit presence of P λ P_{\lambda} ,
the estimates for 𝐠 < λ μ ​ ν {\bf g}_{<\lambda}^{\mu\nu} can be immediately reduced to m = 0 m=0 . 
 
 
 To be precise, the argument above works only for
the spatial derivatives ∇ \nabla , since P < λ P_{<\lambda} truncates the
frequencies of 𝐠 μ ​ ν {\bf g}^{\mu\nu} only with respect to the space variable x x .
However, using the fact that 𝐠 μ ​ ν = ϕ {\bf g}_{\mu\nu}=\phi is a solution of
the wave equation, one can recover the corresponding estimates for the
time derivatives. Let us illustrate this by proving the
estimate 32 32 
 32 
 
 
 This is one of the few estimates with m ≠ 0 m\neq 0 
which we shall actually use. 
( 119 ) with m = 1 m=1 . We assume that we have already proved ( 119 )-( 124 )
for m = 0 m=0 . Then, clearly the derivatives ∇ 2 𝐠 < λ \nabla^{2}{\bf g}_{<\lambda} and

 λ \lambda . It remains to address the derivative

Observe that 
 

 
 
 𝐠 00 < λ ∂ 2 t = 𝐠 α ​ β < λ ∂ α ∂ β + ∑ α = 0 , . . , 3 , β = 1 , . . , 3 𝐠 α ​ β < λ ∂ α ∂ β . {\bf g}^{00}_{<\lambda}\partial^{2}_{t}=\,{\bf g}^{\alpha\beta}_{<\lambda}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}+\sum_{\alpha=0,..,3,\beta=1,..,3}\,{\bf g}^{\alpha\beta}_{<\lambda}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}. 
 
 
 
 
 The desired estimate follows from the condition ( 124 ) with m = 0 m=0 and
the fact that the second term in the previous formula contains at least one
spatial derivative. 
 
 
 
 In view of the above remark we shall make
no distinction between 𝐠 < λ {\bf g}_{<\lambda} and 𝐠 < λ − 1 {\bf g}_{<\lambda}^{-1} in

 ≲ λ 1 2 − 4 ​ ϵ 0 ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L I k ∞ ​ H 1 + γ ≲ λ 1 2 − 4 ​ ϵ 0 . \displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{{\frac{1}{2}}-4\epsilon_{0}}\|\partial\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{I_{k}}H^{1+\gamma}}\lesssim\lambda^{{\frac{1}{2}}-4\epsilon_{0}}.

we sum and integrate on our spacetime slab [ t , t ∗ ] × ℝ 3 [t,t_{*}]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3} .
Observe that, 
 

 
 
 ∂ α ( H α ​ β ​ ∂ β ψ ) = ( ∂ α H α ​ β ) ​ ∂ β ψ + H α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ψ \partial_{\alpha}(H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\psi)=(\partial_{\alpha}H^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\beta}\psi+H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\psi 
 
 
 

 
 
 H α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ψ ​ ∂ t ϕ + H α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ ​ ∂ t ψ \displaystyle H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\psi\,\partial_{t}\phi+H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi\,\partial_{t}\psi 
 = H α ​ β ​ ∂ α ( ∂ t ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ ) + H α ​ β ​ ∂ β ( ∂ t ψ ​ ∂ α ϕ ) \displaystyle=H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}(\partial_{t}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi)+H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}(\partial_{t}\psi\partial_{\alpha}\phi) 
 
 
 
 
 − H α ​ β ​ ( ∂ α ∂ t ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ ) − H α ​ β ​ ( ∂ β ∂ t ψ ​ ∂ α ϕ ) \displaystyle-H^{\alpha\beta}(\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{t}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi)-H^{\alpha\beta}(\partial_{\beta}\partial_{t}\psi\partial_{\alpha}\phi) 
 
 
 
 
 = H α ​ β ​ ∂ α ( ∂ t ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ ) + H α ​ β ​ ∂ β ( ∂ t ψ ​ ∂ α ϕ ) \displaystyle=H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}(\partial_{t}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi)+H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}(\partial_{t}\psi\partial_{\alpha}\phi) 
 
 
 
 
 − H α ​ β ∂ t ( ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ψ ) \displaystyle-H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{t}(\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi) 
 
 
 Thus 
 

 
 
 ∂ α ( H α ​ β ​ ∂ β ψ ) ​ ∂ t ϕ + ∂ α ( H α ​ β ​ ∂ β ϕ ) ​ ∂ t ψ \displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}(H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\psi)\partial_{t}\phi+\partial_{\alpha}(H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi)\partial_{t}\psi 
 = ∂ α ( H α ​ β ​ ∂ t ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ + ∂ t ψ ​ ∂ β ϕ ) \displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha}(H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{t}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi+\partial_{t}\psi\partial_{\beta}\phi) 
 
 
 
 
 − ∂ t ( H α ​ β ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ψ ) + ( ∂ t H α ​ β ) ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ψ \displaystyle-\partial_{t}(H^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi)+(\partial_{t}H^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi 
 
 
 
 
 = ∂ i ( H i ​ β ​ ∂ t ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ + ∂ t ψ ​ ∂ β ϕ ) \displaystyle=\partial_{i}(H^{i\beta}\partial_{t}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi+\partial_{t}\psi\partial_{\beta}\phi) 
 
 
 
 
 + ∂ t ( − H 00 ∂ t ϕ ∂ t ψ + H i ​ j ∂ i ϕ ∂ j ψ ) \displaystyle+\partial_{t}(-H^{00}\partial_{t}\phi\partial_{t}\psi+H^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\psi) 
 
 
 
 
 + ( ∂ t H α ​ β ) ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ψ \displaystyle+(\partial_{t}H^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi 
 
 
 Integrating in the region [ t , t ∗ ] × 𝐑 n [t,t_{*}]\times{\bf R}^{n} 
we derive the identity, 
 

 
 
 ∫ Σ t ( − H 00 ∂ t ϕ ∂ t ψ + H i ​ j ∂ i ϕ ∂ j ψ ) = − ∫ t t ∗ ∫ Σ τ ( − ∂ t ϕ P f + ∂ t ψ F + ∂ t ( H α ​ β ) ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ψ ) . \int_{\Sigma_{t}}\bigg(-H^{00}\partial_{t}\phi\partial_{t}\psi+H^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\psi\bigg)=-\int_{t}^{t_{*}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\bigg(-\partial_{t}\phi\,Pf+\partial_{t}\psi\,F+\partial_{t}(H^{\alpha\beta}\,)\partial_{\alpha}\phi\,\partial_{\beta}\psi\bigg). 
 
 
 Therefore, 
 

 
 
 ‖ ∂ ψ ⁡ ( t ) ‖ L 2 ≤ ‖ P ​ ∂ t ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ​ L x ∞ ​ ‖ f ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ′ ​ L x 1 + C ​ ‖ ∂ H ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] 1 ​ L x ∞ ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 ​ ‖ ∂ ψ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 \|\partial\psi(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|P\,\partial_{t}\phi\|_{L^{q}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\|f\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{1}_{x}}+C\|\partial H\|_{L^{1}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\partial\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}}\|\partial\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}} 
 
 
 We recall that according to
our assumption ‖ P ​ ∂ t ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ​ L x ∞ ≤ M ​ ‖ w ‖ ℋ t ≤ M \|P\,\partial_{t}\phi\|_{L^{q}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq M\|w\|_{{\cal H}_{t}}\leq M .
Also according to the energy estimate,
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 ≤ 2 ​ ‖ w ‖ ℋ t ≤ 2 \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}}\leq 2\|w\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{t}}\leq 2 .
Therefore, 
 
 
 

 
 
 ‖ ∂ ψ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 ≤ M ​ ‖ f ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ′ ​ L x 1 + C ​ ‖ ∂ H ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] 1 ​ L x ∞ ​ ‖ ∂ ψ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 \|\partial\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}}\leq M\|f\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{1}_{x}}+C\|\partial H\|_{L^{1}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\partial\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}} 
 
 
 and therefore, since C ​ ‖ ∂ H ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] 1 ​ L x ∞ ≤ 1 2 C\|\partial H\|_{L^{1}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\frac{1}{2} , we conclude that, 
 

 
 
 ‖ ∂ ψ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 ≤ 2 ​ M ​ ‖ f ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ′ ​ L x 1 \|\partial\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}}\leq 2M\|f\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{1}_{x}} 
 
 
 as desired. 
 
 
 To prove the Strichartz estimate for
the spatial derivatives we rely
on the proof, given above, for P ​ ∂ t ϕ P\,\partial_{t}\phi .
We thus assume that the estimate ( 166 )
holds true for P ​ ∂ t ϕ P\,\partial_{t}\phi with a universal constant M M . 
 
 
 To estimate ‖ P ​ ∂ k ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ​ L x ∞ \|P\,\partial_{k}\phi\|_{L^{q}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}} it suffices
to estimate the integral,
 ℐ = ∫ [ 0 , t ∗ ] × 𝐑 3 P ​ ∂ k ϕ ​ f ​ 𝑑 t ​ 𝑑 x {\cal I}=\int_{[0,t_{*}]\times{\bf R}^{3}}P\,\partial_{k}\phi\,fdtdx for
functions f f with
 ‖ f ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ′ ​ L x ∞ ≤ 1 . \|f\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq 1. Let ψ \psi 
verify the equation □ ¯ H ​ ψ = P ​ f \bar{\square}_{H}\psi=Pf with
 ψ ⁡ ( t ∗ ) = ∂ t ψ ⁡ ( t ∗ ) = 0 \psi(t_{*})=\partial_{t}\psi(t_{*})=0 .
Integrating by parts as before we infer that 
 

 
 
 ℐ = ∫ [ 0 , t ∗ ] × 𝐑 3 ∂ k ϕ ​ □ ¯ H ​ ψ \displaystyle{\cal I}=\int_{[0,t_{*}]\times{\bf R}^{3}}\partial_{k}\phi\,\bar{\square}_{H}\psi 
 = ∫ Σ 0 H 0 ​ β ​ ( ∂ k ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ + ∂ k ψ ​ ∂ β ϕ ) \displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{0}}H^{0\beta}\bigg(\partial_{k}\phi\,\partial_{\beta}\psi+\partial_{k}\psi\,\partial_{\beta}\phi\bigg) 
 
 
 
 
 − ∫ [ 0 , t ∗ ] × 𝐑 3 ( □ ¯ H ϕ ∂ k ψ − ( ∂ k H α ​ β ) ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ψ ) \displaystyle-\int_{[0,t_{*}]\times{\bf R}^{3}}\bigg(\bar{\square}_{H}\phi\,\partial_{k}\psi-(\partial_{k}H^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi\bigg) 
 
 
 Once again 
 

 
 
 | ∫ [ 0 , t ∗ ] × 𝐑 3 □ ¯ H ​ ϕ ​ ∂ k ψ | ≤ C ​ ‖ ∂ H ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] 1 ​ L x ∞ ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 ​ ‖ ∂ ψ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 |\int_{[0,t_{*}]\times{\bf R}^{3}}\bar{\square}_{H}\phi\,\partial_{k}\psi|\leq C\|\partial H\|_{L^{1}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\partial\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}}\|\partial\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}} 
 
 
 Also, 
 

 
 
 ∫ Σ 0 H 0 ​ β ​ ( ∂ k ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ + ∂ k ψ ​ ∂ β ϕ ) ≤ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ L 2 ​ ‖ ∂ ψ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 . \int_{\Sigma_{0}}H^{0\beta}\bigg(\partial_{k}\phi\,\partial_{\beta}\psi+\partial_{k}\psi\,\partial_{\beta}\phi\bigg)\leq\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}}. 
 
 
 The energy estimate ( 153 ) gives
 ‖ ∂ ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 ≤ 2 ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ L 2 \|\partial\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}}\leq 2\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{L^{2}} .
According to the lemma 166 we have, 
 

 
 
 ‖ ∂ ψ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] ∞ ​ L x 2 ≤ 2 ​ M ​ ‖ f ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ′ ​ L x 1 . \|\partial\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{2}_{x}}\leq 2M\|f\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{1}_{x}}. 
 
 
 Observe that the M M in lemma 166 depends only
on the Strichartz estimate ( 155 ) for P ​ ∂ t ϕ P\,\partial_{t}\phi 
which we have already proved.
Therefore, 
 

 
 
 | ℐ | ≤ C ​ M ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( 1 + ‖ ∂ H ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] 1 ​ L x ∞ ) ​ ‖ f ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ′ ​ L x 1 ≤ C ​ M ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ L 2 |{\cal I}|\leq CM\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{L^{2}}(1+\|\partial H\|_{L^{1}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}})\|f\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{1}_{x}}\leq CM\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{L^{2}} 
 
 
 which implies,
 ‖ P ​ ∂ a ϕ ‖ L [ 0 , t ∗ ] q ​ L x ∞ ≤ C ​ M ​ ‖ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( 0 ) ‖ L 2 \|P\,\partial_{a}\phi\|_{L^{q}_{[0,t_{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq CM\|\partial\phi(0)\|_{L^{2}} 
as desired.
   
 
 
 
 8.37. Commutator lemma 

 
 We conclude this section by presenting the proof of lemma 7.1 
from section 21 .
Recall that the definition of the exterior region Ext  t = { u ≤ t / 2 } \mbox{Ext\,}_{t}=\{u\leq t/2\} . 
 
 
 Lemma 8.38 . 
 
 Consider a vectorfield X = ∑ i X i ∂ i X=\sum_{i}X^{i}\partial_{i} vanishing on the complement
of the exterior
region Ext  t \mbox{Ext\,}_{t} of Σ t \Sigma_{t} and P P the standard Littlewood-Paley projection on frequencies of size
1. Then, for arbitrary scalar functions
 f f we have the inequality: 
 

 
 (169) 
 
 ‖ [ P , X ] ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( Ext  t ) ≲ sup i , j ‖ ∂ i X j ‖ L ∞ ​ ( Ext  t ) ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) \|[P,X]f\|_{L^{2}(\mbox{Ext\,}_{t})}\lesssim\sup_{i,j}\|\partial_{i}X^{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mbox{Ext\,}_{t})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof 
First observe, by expanding X = X j ∂ j X=X^{j}\partial_{j} relative to our system of our
coordinates on Σ t \Sigma_{t} , that [ P , X ] = [ P ∂ j , X j ] − P ( ∂ j X j ) [P,X]=[P\partial_{j},X^{j}]-P(\partial_{j}X^{j}) .
We shall denote P j = P ∂ j P_{j}=P\partial_{j} , the modified cut-off of the unit frequencies.
In what follows, the roles of P P and P j P_{j} are identical.
The convolution kernels of P , P j P,P_{j} are represented by the smooth functions
 P ​ ( x ) , P j ​ ( x ) P(x),P_{j}(x) verifying the condition that
 | P ⁡ ( x ) | , | P j ​ ( x ) | ≲ | x | − k |P(x)|,|P_{j}(x)|\lesssim|x|^{-k} for any k > 0 k>0 and | x | ≥ 1 |x|\geq 1 .
In particular, for any functions w , v w,v 
 

 
 
 v \displaystyle v 
 = ∫ Σ t P ⁡ ( x − y ) ​ ( w ⁡ ( y ) − w ⁡ ( x ) ) ​ v ​ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 y \displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}P(x-y)\big(w(y)-w(x)\big)v(y)\,dy 
 
 
 
 
 = − ∫ 0 1 ∫ Σ t P ( x − y ) ( x − y ) i ∂ i w ( τ x + ( 1 − τ ) y ) v ( y ) d y d τ \displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}P(x-y)(x-y)^{i}\partial_{i}w(\tau x+(1-\tau)y)v(y)\,dy\,d\tau 
 
 
 As a consequence, 
 

 
 (170) 
 
 ‖ [ P , w ] ​ v ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) ≲ ‖ ∇ w ‖ L ∞ ​ ( Ext  t ) ​ ‖ v ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) \|[P,w]v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim\|\nabla w\|_{L^{\infty}(\mbox{Ext\,}_{t})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})} 
 
 
 Similar inequality also holds for P j P_{j} . 
 
 
 We shall show that 
 

 
 
 ‖ [ P j , X j ] ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) + ‖ P ⁡ ( ( ∂ j X j ) ​ f ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) ≲ sup i , j ‖ ∂ i X j ‖ L ∞ ​ ( Ext  t ) ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) \|[P_{j},X^{j}]f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}+\|P\big((\partial_{j}X^{j})f\big)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim\sup_{i,j}\|\partial_{i}X^{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mbox{Ext\,}_{t})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})} 
 
 
 Since all X j X^{j} vanish outside of Ext  t \mbox{Ext\,}_{t} and P P is a bounded operator
on L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) L^{2}(\Sigma_{t}) , we can easily estimate the second term, 
 

 
 
 ‖ P ⁡ ( ( ∂ j X j ) ​ f ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) ≲ sup i , j ‖ ∂ i X j ‖ L ∞ ​ ( Ext  t ) ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) \|P\big((\partial_{j}X^{j})f\big)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim\sup_{i,j}\|\partial_{i}X^{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mbox{Ext\,}_{t})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})} 
 
 
 According to ( 170 ) we also have 
 

 
 
 ‖ [ P j , X j ] ​ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) ≲ sup i , j ‖ ∂ i X j ‖ L ∞ ​ ( Ext  t ) ​ ‖ f ‖ L 2 ​ ( Σ t ) \|[P_{j},X^{j}]f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim\sup_{i,j}\|\partial_{i}X^{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mbox{Ext\,}_{t})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}
